# Palmyra

This article is about the ancient city of Palmyra. For the modern city, also known as Tadmur, see [Palmyra (modern)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyra_\(modern\)). For other uses, see [Palmyra (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyra_\(disambiguation\)).

**Palmyra**[^cite_note-1] is an ancient city in central [Syria](7515849.md). It is located in the eastern part of the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant), and archaeological finds date back to the [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) period, and documents first mention the city in the early second millennium BCE. Palmyra changed hands on a number of occasions between different empires before becoming a subject of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) in the first century CE.

The city grew wealthy from [trade caravans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caravan_\(travellers\)); the Palmyrenes became renowned as merchants who established colonies along the [Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road) and operated throughout the Roman Empire. Palmyra's wealth enabled the construction of monumental projects, such as the [Great Colonnade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Colonnade_at_Palmyra), the [Temple of Bel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_of_Bel), and the distinctive tower tombs. Ethnically, the Palmyrenes combined elements of [Amorites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amorites), [Arameans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arameans), and [Arabs](2185.md). Socially structured around kinship and clans, Palmyra's inhabitants spoke [Palmyrene Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_Aramaic), a variety of [Western Middle Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramaic#Middle_Aramaic), while using [Koine Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koine_Greek) for commercial and diplomatic purposes. The [Hellenistic period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_period) of [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia) influenced the [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Syria) of Palmyra, which produced distinctive art and architecture that combined different Mediterranean traditions. The city's inhabitants worshiped [local Semitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaanite_religion), [Mesopotamian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Mesopotamian_religion), and [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_pre-Islamic_Arabia) deities.

By the third century, Palmyra had become a prosperous regional center. It reached the apex of its power in the 260s, when the Palmyrene King [Odaenathus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odaenathus) defeated the [Sasanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_Empire) emperor [Shapur I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shapur_I). The king was succeeded by queen regent [Zenobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zenobia), who rebelled against Rome and established the [Palmyrene Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_Empire). In 273, Roman emperor [Aurelian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurelian) levelled the city, which was later restored by [Diocletian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocletian) at a reduced size. The Palmyrenes converted to [Christianity](5211.md) during the fourth century and to [Islam](6037917.md) in the centuries following the conquest by the seventh-century [Rashidun Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun_Caliphate), after which the Palmyrene and Greek languages were replaced by [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic).

Before 273 CE, Palmyra enjoyed autonomy and was attached to the Roman [province of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_\(Roman_province\)), having its political organization influenced by the [Greek city-state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polis) model during the first two centuries CE. The city became a [Roman *colonia*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonia_\(Roman\)) during the third century, leading to the incorporation of Roman governing institutions, before becoming a monarchy in 260. Following its razing in 273, Palmyra became a minor center under the [Byzantines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) and later empires. Its destruction by the [Timurids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_Empire) in 1400 reduced it to a small village. Under [French Mandatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Mandate_of_Syria) rule in 1932, the inhabitants were moved into the new village of [Tadmur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tadmur), and the ancient site became available for excavations. During the [Syrian civil war](30741795.md) in 2015, the [Islamic State](9087364.md) captured Palmyra and destroyed large parts of the ancient city, which was recaptured by the [Syrian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Arab_Armed_Forces) on 2 March 2017. It was then captured by the [Syrian Free Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Free_Army) after the fall of the [Assad government](57134800.md) in December 2024.

## Etymology

Records of the name "Tadmor" date from the early second millennium BC;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connor1988\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidx1l_MfEV6kkCpgPA238_238\]-2] 18th-century BC tablets from [Mari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mari,_Syria) written in [cuneiform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuneiform_script) record the name as "Ta-ad-mi-ir", while Assyrian inscriptions of the 11th century BC record it as "Ta-ad-mar".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELimet1977\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid-5o3AAAAIAAJpgPA104_104\]-3] [Palmyrene inscriptions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_inscriptions) themselves showed two variants of the name; *TDMR* (i.e., Tadmar) and *TDMWR* (i.e., Tadmor).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBubeník1989\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidDT98F5CFtQsCpgPA229_229\]-4][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWolfensohn2016\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidm2WSDQAAQBAJpgPT118_118\]-5] The etymology of the name is unclear; the standard interpretation, supported by [Albert Schultens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Schultens), connects it to the Semitic word for "[date palm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Date_palm)", *tamar* ([תמר](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%D7%AA%D7%9E%D7%A8)),[^cite_note-10][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECharnock1859\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsbub_gb_I2BulY4WvsYCpagen210_200\]-8][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connor1988\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidx1l_MfEV6kkCpgPA235_235\]-9] thus referring to the palm trees that surrounded the city.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connor1988\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidx1l_MfEV6kkCpgPA235_235\]-9]

The Greek name Παλμύρα (Latinized *Palmyra*) was first recorded by [Pliny the Elder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pliny_the_Elder) in the first century AD.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connor1988\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidx1l_MfEV6kkCpgPA248_248\]-11] It was used throughout the [Greco-Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Roman) world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECharnock1859\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsbub_gb_I2BulY4WvsYCpagen210_200\]-8] It is generally believed that "Palmyra" derives from "Tadmor" and linguists have presented two possibilities; one view holds that Palmyra was an alteration of Tadmor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECharnock1859\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsbub_gb_I2BulY4WvsYCpagen210_200\]-8] According to the suggestion by Schultens, "Palmyra" could have arisen as a corruption of "Tadmor", via an unattested form "Talmura", changed to "Palmura" by the influence of the Latin word *[palma](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/palma)* (date "[palm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arecaceae)"),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connor1988\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidx1l_MfEV6kkCpgPA238_238\]-2] in reference to the city's palm trees, then the name reached its final form "Palmyra".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECharnock1859\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsbub_gb_I2BulY4WvsYCpagen211_201\]-12] The second view, supported by some philologists, such as [Jean Starcky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Starcky), holds that Palmyra is a translation of "Tadmor" (assuming that it meant palm), which had derived from the Greek word for palm, "*palame*".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connor1988\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidx1l_MfEV6kkCpgPA238_238\]-2][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connor1988\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidx1l_MfEV6kkCpgPA235_235\]-9]

An alternative suggestion connects the name to the [Syriac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_language) *tedmurtā* (ܬܕܡܘܪܬܐ) "miracle", hence *tedmurtā* "object of wonder", from the root *dmr* "to wonder"; this possibility was mentioned favourably by [Franz Altheim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franz_Altheim) and Ruth Altheim-Stiehl (1973), but rejected by Jean Starcky (1960) and Michael Gawlikowski (1974).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connor1988\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidx1l_MfEV6kkCpgPA248_248\]-11] [Michael Patrick O'Connor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Patrick_O'Connor) (1988) suggested that the names "Palmyra" and "Tadmor" originated in the [Hurrian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurrian_language).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connor1988\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidx1l_MfEV6kkCpgPA238_238\]-2] As evidence, he cited the inexplicability of alterations to the theorized roots of both names (represented in the addition of *-d-* to *tamar* and *-ra-* to *palame*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connor1988\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidx1l_MfEV6kkCpgPA235_235\]-9] According to this theory, "Tadmor" derives from the Hurrian word *tad* ("to love") with the addition of the typical Hurrian [mid vowel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid_vowel) rising (mVr) [formant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formant) *mar*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connor1988\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidx1l_MfEV6kkCpgPA236_236\]-13] Similarly, according to this theory, "Palmyra" derives from the Hurrian word *pal* ("to know") using the same mVr formant (*mar*).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connor1988\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidx1l_MfEV6kkCpgPA236_236\]-13]

## Region and city layout

See also: [Valley of the Tombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valley_of_the_Tombs)



The northern Palmyrene mountain belt



Palmyra's landmarks

The city of Palmyra lies 215 km (134 mi) northeast of the Syrian capital, [Damascus](8914.md);[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuntern2010\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidcyGAqI9SHPECpgPA433_433\]-14] along with an expanded hinterland of several settlements, farms, and forts, the city forms part of the region known as [Palmyrena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrena) or Palmyrene.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIntagliata2018\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidl6RTDwAAQBAJpgPA1_1\]-15] The city is located in an [oasis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oasis) surrounded by [palms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Date_palm) (of which twenty varieties have been reported).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connor1988\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidx1l_MfEV6kkCpgPA235_235\]-9][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA56_56\]-16] Two mountain ranges overlook the city - the northern [Palmyrene mountain belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleppo_plateau) from the north and the southern Palmyrene mountains from the southwest.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIzumi199519-17] In the south and the east Palmyra is exposed to the [Syrian Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Desert).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIzumi199519-17] A small [wadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wadi), al-Qubur (Valley of the Tombs), crosses the area, flowing from the western hills past the city before disappearing in the eastern gardens of the oasis.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZuchowska2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidFgML1EdUiWUCpgPA229_229\]-18] South of the wadi is a spring, [Efqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efqa).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA17_17\]-19] Pliny the Elder described the town in the 70s AD as famous for its desert location, for the richness of its soil,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung2003\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidE5yCAgAAQBAJpgPA124_124\]-20] and for the springs surrounding it, which made agriculture and herding possible.[^cite_note-22][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung2003\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidE5yCAgAAQBAJpgPA124_124\]-20]

### Layout

Palmyra began as a small Neolithic settlement near the Efqa spring on the southern bank of Wadi al-Qubur.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETomlinson2003\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidb9-KAgAAQBAJpgPA204_204\]-23] The much later Hellenistic settlement of Palmyra was also located near the Efqa spring on the southern bank of Wadi al-Qubur.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETomlinson2003\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidb9-KAgAAQBAJpgPA204_204\]-23] It had its residences expanding to the wadi's northern bank during the first century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZuchowska2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidFgML1EdUiWUCpgPA229_229\]-18] Although the city's walls at the time of Zenobia originally enclosed an extensive area on both banks of the wadi,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZuchowska2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidFgML1EdUiWUCpgPA229_229\]-18] the walls rebuilt during Aurelian's reign surrounded only the northern-bank section.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJuchniewicz2013194-24][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZuchowska2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidFgML1EdUiWUCpgPA229_229\]-18] Most of the city's monumental projects were built on the wadi's northern bank,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZuchowska2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidFgML1EdUiWUCpgPA230_230\]-25] among them is the [Temple of Bel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_of_Bel), on a [tell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tell_\(archaeology\)) which was the site of an earlier temple (known as the Hellenistic temple).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA63_63\]-26] However, excavation supports the theory that the tell was originally located on the southern bank, and the wadi was diverted south of the tell to incorporate the temple into Palmyra's late first and early second century urban organization on the north bank.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZuchowska2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidFgML1EdUiWUCpgPA231_231\]-27]

Also north of the wadi was the [Great Colonnade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Colonnade_at_Palmyra), Palmyra's 1.1-kilometre-long (0.68 mi) main street,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrawford1990\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidgGMc04ge4yoCpgPA123_123\]-28] which extended from the Temple of Bel in the east,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECotterman2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid0IKiAgAAQBAJpgPA17_17\]-29] to the Funerary Temple no.86 in the city's western part.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGawlikowski2005\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidoZcr7SzzVYYCpgPA55_55\]-30][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBall2002\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid73-JAgAAQBAJpgPA364_364\]-31] It had a [monumental arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arch_of_Triumph_\(Palmyra\)) in its eastern section,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDe_Laborde1837\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidLwAcAQAAIAAJpgPA239_239\]-32] and a [tetrapylon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tetrapylon) stands in the center.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERicca2007\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid3SapTk5iGDkCpgPA295_295\]-33] The Baths of Diocletian were on the left side of the colonnade.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA124_124\]-34] Nearby were residences,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrijvers1976\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidmuQ3AAAAIAAJpgPA5_5\]-35] the [Temple of Baalshamin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_of_Baalshamin),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA22_22\]-36] and the Byzantine churches, which include "Basilica IV", Palmyra's largest church.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMajcherek2013\[httpdigiubuni-heidelbergdediglitstudia_palmyrenskie20130256sid869feb64802fc695d2e489a1718e1fbc_254\]-37] The church is dated to the [Justinian age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire_under_the_Justinian_dynasty),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMajcherek2013\[httpdigiubuni-heidelbergdediglitstudia_palmyrenskie20130258sid869feb64802fc695d2e489a1718e1fbc_256\]-38] its columns are estimated to be 7 metres (23 ft) high, and its base measured 27.5 by 47.5 metres (90 by 156 ft).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMajcherek2013\[httpdigiubuni-heidelbergdediglitstudia_palmyrenskie20130256sid869feb64802fc695d2e489a1718e1fbc_254\]-37]

The Temple of [Nabu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabu) and the [Roman theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Theatre_at_Palmyra) were built on the colonnade's southern side.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarterDunstonThomas2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_R-I_Gx5OgQCpgPA208_208\]-39] Behind the theater were a small senate building and the large [agora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agora), with the remains of a *[triclinium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triclinium)* (banquet room) and the Tariff Court.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDarke2006\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidHuXAHGDSjNICpgPA240_240\]-40] A cross street at the western end of the colonnade leads to the [Camp of Diocletian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_of_Diocletian),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrawford1990\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidgGMc04ge4yoCpgPA123_123\]-28][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeattiePepper2001\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid9iCfkTe8v2ECpgRA2-PA290_290\]-41] built by [Sosianus Hierocles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sossianus_Hierocles) (the Roman governor of Syria in the reign of [Diocletian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocletian)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidz_IBAwAAQBAJpgPA216_216\]-42] Nearby are the [Temple of Al-lāt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_of_Al-Lat) and the Damascus Gate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrowning1979180-43]

## Society

### Demographic history

The earliest known inhabitants were the [Amorites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amorites) in the early second millennium BC,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBen-YehoshuaBorowitzHanus2012\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidS_Jjwq206QoCpgPT26_26\]-44] and by the end of the millennium, [Arameans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arameans) were mentioned as inhabiting the area.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreene2001\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidamlXOOaSuLMCpgPA17_17\]-45][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECotterman2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid0IKiAgAAQBAJpgPA4_4\]-46] [Arabs](2185.md) arrived in the city in the late first millennium BC,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA278_278\]-47] as evidenced by the presence of [Zabdibel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zabdibel?action=edit\&redlink=1) on the side of the [Seleucids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucids) in the [battle of Raphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Raphia) (217 BC). He was mentioned as the commander of "the Arabs and neighbouring tribes to the number of ten thousands";[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaizer2017\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidC7Q1DwAAQBAJpgPA34lpgPA34_34\]-48] Zabdibel and his men were not actually identified as Palmyrenes in the texts, but the name "Zabdibel" is a Palmyrene name leading to the conclusion that he hailed from Palmyra.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA359_359\]-49] The Arab newcomers were assimilated by the earlier inhabitants, used Palmyrene as a mother tongue,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA19_19\]-50] and formed a significant segment of the aristocracy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELuxenberg2007\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid227GhaeKYl4CpgPA11_11\]-51]

The classical city also had a Jewish community; [Palmyrene inscriptions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_inscriptions) from the [Beit She'arim necropolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beit_She'arim_necropolis) in [Lower Galilee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Galilee) confirm the burial of Palmyrene Jews.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETeixidor2005\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidoZcr7SzzVYYCpgPA209_209\]-52] Greeks existence in the city was limited during the Roman period. Occasionally and rarely, members of the Palmyrene families took Greek names while ethnic Greeks were few; the majority of people with Greek names, who did not belong to one of the city's families, were freed slaves.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERostovtzeff1932\[httpsarchiveorgstreamRostCaravanCitiesRost_Caravan_Citiespagen171mode2up_133\]-53] The Palmyrenes seem to have considered the Greeks foreigners, and restricted their settlement in the city.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERostovtzeff1932\[httpsarchiveorgstreamRostCaravanCitiesRost_Caravan_Citiespagen171mode2up_133\]-53]

At its height during the reign of Zenobia (around 270), Palmyra had more than 200,000 residents.[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECotterman2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid0IKiAgAAQBAJpgPA5_5\]-56] The city's population shrank following the city's integration to the Roman Empire following the rebellion of Zenobia and during the succeeding [Umayyad Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate), Palmyra was mainly inhabited by the [Banu Kalb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Kalb).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrabarHolodKnustadTrousdale1978\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidQMSCNQlywSsCpgPA156_156\]-57] [Benjamin of Tudela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_of_Tudela) recorded the existence of 2000 Jews in the city during the twelfth century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA192_192\]-58] Palmyra declined after its destruction by [Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur) in 1400,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKitto1837\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidxMBWAAAAcAAJpgPA341_341\]-59] and was a village of 6,000 inhabitants at the beginning of the 20th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBelnapHaeri1997\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidl03h94vqCQMCpgPA21_21\]-60]

### Ethnic identity of classical Palmyra

Palmyra's population was a mixture of the different peoples inhabiting the city,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA18_18\]-61][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETeixidor1979\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidTccUAAAAIAAJpgPR9_9\]-62] which is seen in Aramaic, Arabic and Amorite names of Palmyrene clans,[^cite_note-64][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA67_67\]-63] but the ethnicity of Palmyra is a matter of debate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVeyne2017\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidqZUtDwAAQBAJpgPA60_60\]-65]

Some scholars, such as Andrew M. Smith II, consider ethnicity a concept related to modern nationalism, and prefer not to describe the Palmyrenes with ethnic designations they themselves did not know, concluding that there is a lack of evidence regarding what ethnicity the Palmyrenes perceived themselves.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA36_36\],_37-66] On the other hand, many scholars, such as Eivind Seland, contend that a distinctive Palmyrene ethnicity is apparent in the available contemporary evidence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeland2013381-67] The second century work *[De Munitionibus Castrorum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_Munitionibus_Castrorum)* mentioned the Palmyrenes as a *natio*, the Latin equivalent of the Greek ἔθνος (éthnos).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacdonald20099-68] Seland noted the epigraphic evidence left by the Palmyrenes outside the city.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeland2013381-67]

[Palmyrene inscriptions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_inscriptions) reveal the existence of a real diaspora satisfying the three criteria set by the sociologist [Rogers Brubaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rogers_Brubaker).[^cite_note-70][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeland2013382-69] Palmyrene diaspora members always made clear their Palmyrene origin and used the Palmyrene language, and maintained their distinct religion even when the host society's religion was close to that of Palmyra. Seland concluded that in the case of Palmyra, the people perceived themselves different from their neighbours and a real Palmyrene ethnicity existed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeland2013383-71] Aside from the existence of a Palmyrene ethnicity, Aramean or Arab are the two main ethnic designations debated by historians;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVeyne2017\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidqZUtDwAAQBAJpgPA60_60\]-65] Javier Teixidor stated, "Palmyra was an Aramaean city and it is a mistake to consider it as an Arab town", while Yasamin Zahran criticized this statement and argued that the inhabitants considered themselves Arabs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZahran200335,_36-72] In practice, according to several scholars such as Udo Hartmann and Michael Sommer, the citizenry of Palmyra were mainly the result of Arab and Aramaean tribes merging into a unity with a corresponding consciousness; they thought and acted as Palmyrenes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHartmann2001\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidBdcHK8Ll1jMCpgPA88_88\]-73][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESommer2018146-74]

### Language

Further information: [Palmyrene Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_Aramaic) and [Palmyrene alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_alphabet)

Alphabetic inscription on stone

Alphabetic inscription in Palmyrene alphabet

Until the late third century, Palmyrenes spoke [Palmyrene Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_Aramaic) and used the [Palmyrene alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_alphabet).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeyer1986\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidpZ53zpMQNLECpgPA28_28\]-75][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHealey1990\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid0_KnI588AnkCpgPA46_46\]-76] The use of [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin) was minimal, but Greek was used by wealthier members of society for commercial and diplomatic purposes,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA280_280\]-77] and it became the dominant language during the Byzantine era.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERicca2007\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid3SapTk5iGDkCpgPA293_293\]-78] There are several theories explaining the disappearance of the Palmyrene language shortly after the campaigns of Aurelian. The linguist Jean Cantineau assumed that Aurelian suppressed all aspects of Palmyrene culture, including the language, but the last [Palmyrene inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_inscriptions) dates to 279/280, after the death of the Roman emperor in 275, thus refuting such a theory.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHartmann201666-79] Many scholars ascribe the disappearance of the language to a change in society resulting from the reorganization of the Eastern Roman frontier following the fall of Zenobia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHartmann201666-79] The archaeologist Karol Juchniewicz ascribed it to a change in the ethnic composition of the city, resulting from the influx of people who did not speak Aramaic, probably a Roman legion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJuchniewicz2013194-24] Hartmann suggested that it was a Palmyrene initiative by nobles allied to Rome attempting to express their loyalty to the emperor; Hartmann noted that Palmyrene disappeared in the written form, and that this does not mean its extinction as spoken language.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHartmann201667-80] After the Arab conquest, Greek was replaced by [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERicca2007\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid3SapTk5iGDkCpgPA293_293\]-78] from which, although the city was surrounded by Bedouins, a Palmyrene dialect evolved.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBelnapHaeri1997\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidl03h94vqCQMCpgPA21_21\]-60]

### Social organization

Bust of a deceased woman, Aqmat

Palmyrene funerary portrait representing Aqmat, a Palmyrene aristocrat

Classical Palmyra was a tribal community, but due to the lack of sources, an understanding of the nature of Palmyrene tribal structure is not possible.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA38_38\]-81] Thirty clans have been documented;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA282_282\]-82] five of which were identified as tribes (*Phylai* [Koine Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koine_Greek_language): Φυλαί, pl. of [*Phyle* Φυλή](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phyle)) comprising several sub-clans.[^cite_note-84][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA24_24\]-83] By the time of [Nero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nero), Palmyra had four tribes, each residing in an area of the city bearing its name.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA25_25\]-85] Three of the tribes were the [Komare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bene_Komare), Mattabol and [Ma'zin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bene_Ma'zin); the fourth tribe is uncertain, but was probably the Mita.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA25_25\]-85][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA74_74\]-86]

In time, the four tribes became highly civic and tribal lines blurred;[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA25_25\]-85] by the second century clan identity lost its importance, and it disappeared during the third century.[^cite_note-89][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA25_25\]-85] Even the four tribes ceased to be important by the third century as only one inscription mentions a tribe after the year 212; instead, aristocrats played the decisive role in the city's social organization.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHartmann201661,_62-90] Women seem to have been active in Palmyra's social and public life. They commissioned inscriptions, buildings or tombs, and in certain cases, held administrative offices. Offerings to gods in the names of women are documented.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoyland2001\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsARABIAANDTHEARABSFromTheBronzeAgeToTheComingOfIslamRobertGHoylandpagen143_132\]-91]

The last Palmyrene inscription of 279/280 refers to the honoring of a citizen by the Maththabolians,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHartmann201666-79] which indicates that the tribal system still carried weight after the fall of Zenobia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIntagliata201899-92] A noticeable change is the lack of development of aristocratic residences, and no important public buildings were constructed by locals, indicating that the elite diminished following the campaign of Aurelian. The social change and the reduction of the aristocratic elite is hard to explain. It could be a result of the aristocracy suffering many casualties in the war against Rome, or fleeing to the countryside. According to historian [Emanuele Intagliata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emanuele_Intagliata?action=edit\&redlink=1), the change can be ascribed to the Roman reorganization following Zenobia's fall, as Palmyra ceased to be a rich caravan city and became a frontier fortress, leading the inhabitants to focus on satisfying the needs of a garrison instead of providing the empire with luxurious oriental items. Such a change in functions would have made the city less attractive for an aristocratic elite.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIntagliata201844,_45-93]

Palmyra benefited from [Umayyad rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate), since its role as a frontier city ended and the East-West trade route was restored, leading to the re-emergence of a merchant class. Palmyra's loyalty to the Umayyads led to an aggressive military retaliation from their successors, the [Abbasid Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Caliphate), and the city diminished in size, losing its merchant class.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIntagliata2018113-94] Following its destruction by [Timur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timur), Palmyra maintained the life of a small settlement until its relocation in 1932.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpeake1996\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidR44VRnNCzAYCpgPA568_568\]-95]

### Culture

The scarce artifacts found in the city dating to the Bronze Age reveal that, culturally, Palmyra was most affiliated with western Syria.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBielińska1997\[httpdigiubuni-heidelbergdediglitstudia_palmyrenskie19970044_44\]-96] [Classical](252905.md) Palmyra had a distinctive culture,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillar1993\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidIA-YlZqHv90CpgPA246_246\]-97] based on a [local Semitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Syria) tradition,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA281_281\]-98] and influenced by Greece and Rome.[^cite_note-100][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwell2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidDQgmOZlsEWcCpgPA33_33\]-101] To appear better integrated into the Roman Empire, some Palmyrenes adopted Greco-Roman names, either alone or in addition to a second native name.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYon2002\[httpsbooksopeneditionorgifpo3763_59\]-102] The extent of Greek influence on Palmyra's culture is debated.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrade2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid4ROhAQAAQBAJpgPT264_264\]-103] Scholars interpreted the Palmyrenes' Greek practices differently; many see those characters as a superficial layer over a local essence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrade2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid4ROhAQAAQBAJpgPT263_263\]-104] Palmyra's senate was an example; although Palmyrene texts written in Greek described it as a "[boule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boule_\(ancient_Greece\))" (a Greek institution), the senate was a gathering of non-elected tribal elders (a Near-Eastern assembly tradition).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBall2002\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid73-JAgAAQBAJpgPA446_446\]-105] Others view Palmyra's culture as a fusion of local and Greco-Roman traditions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillar2007\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidmimRtU4vtDwCpgPA108_108\]-106]



Palmyrene loculi (burial chambers) reassembled in the [İstanbul Archaeological Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%B0stanbul_Archaeology_Museums)



Palmyrene mummy

The culture of [Persia](965233.md) influenced Palmyrene military tactics, dress and court ceremonies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBall2002\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid73-JAgAAQBAJpgPA86_86\]-107] Palmyra had no large libraries or publishing facilities, and it lacked an intellectual movement characteristic of other Eastern cities such as [Edessa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edessa) or Antioch.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBall2002\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid73-JAgAAQBAJpgPA79_79\]-108] Although Zenobia opened her court to academics, the only notable scholar documented was [Cassius Longinus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassius_Longinus_\(philosopher\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBall2002\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid73-JAgAAQBAJpgPA79_79\]-108]

Palmyra had a large [agora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agora).[^cite_note-110] However, unlike the Greek Agoras (public gathering places shared with public buildings), Palmyra's agora resembled an Eastern [caravanserai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caravanserai) more than a hub of public life.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERaja2012\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid0VK6uPyhFLACpgPA198_198\]-111][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBall2002\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid73-JAgAAQBAJpgPA296_296\]-112] The Palmyrenes buried their dead in elaborate family mausoleums,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChapot2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidlDmEAgAAQBAJpgPA168_168\]-113] most with interior walls forming rows of burial chambers ([loculi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loculus_\(architecture\))) in which the dead, lying at full length, were placed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBenzelGraffRakicWatts2010\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidgBCHchG6qDACpgPA106_106\]-114][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvansKevorkian2000\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidCMaY0_y6gK0CpgPA115_115\]-115] A [relief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relief) of the person interred formed part of the wall's decoration, acting as a headstone.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvansKevorkian2000\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidCMaY0_y6gK0CpgPA115_115\]-115] [Sarcophagi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarcophagi) appeared in the late second century and were used in some of the tombs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGawlikowski2005\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidoZcr7SzzVYYCpgPA54_54\]-116] Many burial monuments contained [mummies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mummy) embalmed in a method similar to that used in [Ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egyptian_burial_customs#Mummification).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEColledge197661-117][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWood1753\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidQOYRAwAAQBAJpgPA22_22\]-118]

### Art and architecture

Further information: [Palmyrene funerary reliefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_funerary_reliefs)



Interior of the [Tower of Elahbel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tower_of_Elahbel), in 2010

Although Palmyrene art was related to [that of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_art), it had a distinctive style unique to the middle-Euphrates region.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillar1993\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidIA-YlZqHv90CpgPA329_329\]-119] Palmyrene art is well represented by the [bust reliefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_funerary_reliefs) which seal the openings of its burial chambers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillar1993\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidIA-YlZqHv90CpgPA329_329\]-119] The reliefs emphasized clothing, jewelry and a frontal representation of the person depicted,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillar1993\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidIA-YlZqHv90CpgPA329_329\]-119][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETuck2015\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidPJ_mBQAAQBAJpgPA252_252\]-120] characteristics which can be seen as a forerunner of [Byzantine art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_art).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillar1993\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidIA-YlZqHv90CpgPA329_329\]-119] According to [Michael Rostovtzeff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Rostovtzeff), Palmyra's art was influenced by [Parthian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthian_art).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYarshater1998\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid39XZDnOWUXsCpgPA16_16\]-121] However, the origin of frontality that characterized Palmyrene and Parthian arts is a controversial issue; while Parthian origin has been suggested (by [Daniel Schlumberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Schlumberger)),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrijvers1990\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidz51PhFgWoksCpgPA69_69\]-122] [Michael Avi-Yonah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Avi-Yonah) contends that it was a local Syrian tradition that influenced Parthian art.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHachlili1998\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidcKGpa-FJ3XsCpgPA177_177\]-123] Little painting, and none of the bronze statues of prominent citizens (which stood on brackets on the main columns of the Great Colonnade), have survived.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrong1995168-124] A damaged [frieze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frieze) and other sculptures from the Temple of Bel, many removed to museums in Syria and abroad, suggest the city's public monumental sculpture.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrong1995168-124]

Many surviving funerary busts reached Western museums during the 19th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERomano2006\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidta04lu10P0YCpgPA280_280\]-125] Palmyra provided the most convenient Eastern examples bolstering an art-history controversy at the turn of the 20th century: to what extent Eastern influence on [Roman art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_art) replaced idealized classicism with frontal, hieratic and simplified figures (as believed by [Josef Strzygowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josef_Strzygowski) and others).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrong1995168-124][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFowden2004\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidYKY9MuRTFQ4CpgPA17_17\]-126] This transition is seen as a response to cultural changes in the [Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Roman_Empire), rather than artistic influence from the East.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrong1995168-124] Palmyrene bust reliefs, unlike Roman sculptures, are rudimentary portraits; although many reflect high quality individuality, the majority vary little across figures of similar age and gender.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrong1995168-124]

Like its art, Palmyra's architecture was influenced by the Greco-Roman style, while preserving local elements (best seen in the Temple of Bel).[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA54_54\]-127][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA64_64\]-131] Enclosed by a massive wall flanked with traditional Roman columns,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA64_64\]-131][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERostovtzeff1971\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5lVDl8uqMQCpgPA90_90\]-132] Bel's sanctuary plan was primarily Semitic.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA64_64\]-131] Similar to the [Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple), the sanctuary consisted of a large courtyard with the deity's main shrine off-center against its entrance (a plan preserving elements of the temples of [Ebla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebla) and [Ugarit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ugarit)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA64_64\]-131][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA65_65\]-133]

## Site

### Cemeteries



Valley of Tombs in 2010



The senate



Baths of Diocletian



Temple of [Nabu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabu), in 2010



The statue of Al-lāt (equated with Athena) found in its temple (destroyed in 2015)



The Funerary Temple no.86



Diocletian's walls

West of the ancient walls, the Palmyrenes built a number of large-scale funerary monuments which now form the [Valley of Tombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valley_of_Tombs),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidz_IBAwAAQBAJpgPA218_218\]-134] a one-kilometre-long (0.62 mi) [necropolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Necropolis).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeattiePepper2001\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid9iCfkTe8v2ECpgRA2-PA291_291\]-135] The more than 50 monuments were primarily tower-shaped and up to four stories high.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERichardson200247-136] Towers were replaced by funerary temples in the first half of the second century AD, as the most recent tower is dated to AD 128.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGawlikowski2005\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidoZcr7SzzVYYCpgPA55_55\]-30] The city had other cemeteries in the north, southwest and southeast, where the tombs are primarily [hypogea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypogeum) (underground).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidz_IBAwAAQBAJpgPA219_219\]-137][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidz_IBAwAAQBAJpgPA220_220\]-138]

### Notable structures

#### Public buildings

Further information: [Camp of Diocletian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_of_Diocletian) and [Roman Theatre at Palmyra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Theatre_at_Palmyra)

- **The senate** building is largely ruined.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDarke2006\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidHuXAHGDSjNICpgPA240_240\]-40]

  &#x20;It is a small building that consists of a [peristyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peristyle) courtyard and a chamber that has an [apse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apse) at one end and rows of seats around it.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA282_282\]-82]

- Much of the **Baths of Diocletian** are ruined and do not survive above the level of the foundations.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeattiePepper2001\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid9iCfkTe8v2ECpgRA2-PA288_288\]-139]

  &#x20;The complex's entrance is marked by four massive Egyptian granite columns each 1.3 metres (4 ft 3 in) in diameter, 12.5 metres (41 ft) high and weigh 20 tonnes.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDarke2006\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidHuXAHGDSjNICpgPA240_240\]-40]

  &#x20;Inside, the outline of a bathing pool surrounded by a colonnade of [Corinthian columns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corinthian_column) is still visible in addition to an octagonal room that served as a dressing room containing a drain in its center.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDarke2006\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidHuXAHGDSjNICpgPA240_240\]-40]

  &#x20;[Sossianus Hierocles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sossianus_Hierocles), a governor under Emperor [Diocletian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocletian), claimed to have built the baths, but the building was probably erected in the late second century and Sossianus Hierocles renovated it.

  [\[note 13\]](#cite_note-141)

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHartmann201665-142]

- The **Agora** of Palmyra is part of a complex that also includes the tariff court and the triclinium, built in the second half of the first century AD.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrowning1979157-143]

  &#x20;The agora is a massive 71-by-84-metre (233 by 276 ft) structure with 11 entrances.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDarke2006\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidHuXAHGDSjNICpgPA240_240\]-40]

  &#x20;Inside the agora, 200 columnar bases that used to hold statues of prominent citizens were found.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDarke2006\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidHuXAHGDSjNICpgPA240_240\]-40]

  &#x20;The inscriptions on the bases allowed an understanding of the order by which the statues were grouped; the eastern side was reserved for senators, the northern side for Palmyrene officials, the western side for soldiers and the southern side for caravan chiefs.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDarke2006\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidHuXAHGDSjNICpgPA240_240\]-40]

- The **Tariff Court** is a large rectangular enclosure south of the agora and sharing its northern wall with it.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEButcher2003253-144]

  &#x20;Originally, the entrance of the court was a massive vestibule in its southwestern wall.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEButcher2003253-144]

  &#x20;However, the entrance was blocked by the construction of a defensive wall and the court was entered through three doors from the Agora.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEButcher2003253-144]

  &#x20;The court gained its name by containing a 5-metre (16 ft) stone slab that had the Palmyrene tax law inscribed on it.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeattiePepper2001\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid9iCfkTe8v2ECpgRA2-PA289_289\]-145]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGawlikowski2011420-146]

- The **Triclinium of the Agora** is at the northwestern corner of the Agora and can host up to 40 people.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarterDunstonThomas2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_R-I_Gx5OgQCpgPA209_209\]-147]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEal-AsaadChatonnetYon2005\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidoZcr7SzzVYYCpgPA6_6\]-148]

  &#x20;It is a small 12-by-15-metre (39 by 49 ft) hall decorated with [Greek key motifs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meander_\(art\)) that run in a continuous line halfway up the wall.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTERichardson200246-149]

  &#x20;The building was probably used by the rulers of the city;

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarterDunstonThomas2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_R-I_Gx5OgQCpgPA209_209\]-147]

  &#x20;the French general director of antiquities in Syria, [Henri Seyrig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_Seyrig), proposed that it was a small temple before being turned into a [triclinium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triclinium) or banqueting hall.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEal-AsaadChatonnetYon2005\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidoZcr7SzzVYYCpgPA6_6\]-148]

#### Temples

- The **[Temple of Bel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_of_Bel)** was dedicated in AD 32;

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillar1993\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidIA-YlZqHv90CpgPA323_323\]-150]

  &#x20;it consisted of a large [precinct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_precinct) lined by [porticos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porticos); it had a rectangular shape and was oriented north-south.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGates2003\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid--x-3W2R_QwCpgPA390_390\]-151]

  &#x20;The exterior wall was 205-metre (673 ft) long with a [propylaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propylaea),

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEButcher2003\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidYJPn3-rRjC0CpgPR361_361\]-152]

  &#x20;and the cella stood on a podium in the middle of the enclosure.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTETeixidor1979\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidTccUAAAAIAAJpgPR128_128\]-153]

  &#x20;The temple was destroyed by IS in 2015.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbdulkarim2020115-154]

- The **[Temple of Baalshamin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_of_Baalshamin)** dates to the late 2nd century BC in its earliest phases;

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA276_276\]-155]

  &#x20;its [altar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altar) was built in AD 115,

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA65_65\]-133]

  &#x20;and it was substantially rebuilt in AD 131.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillar1993\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidIA-YlZqHv90CpgPA320_320\]-156]

  &#x20;It consisted of a central cella and two colonnaded courtyards north and south of the central structure.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidz_IBAwAAQBAJpgPA214_214\]-157]

  &#x20;A [vestibule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vestibule_\(architecture\)) consisting of six columns preceded the cella which had its side walls decorated with pilasters in Corinthian order.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidz_IBAwAAQBAJpgPA217_217\]-158]

  &#x20;IS destroyed the temple in 2015.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbdulkarim2020115-154]

- The **[Temple of Nabu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_of_Nabu_\(Palmyra\)?action=edit\&redlink=1)** is largely ruined.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDarke2006\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidHuXAHGDSjNICpgPA241_241\]-159]

  &#x20;The temple was Eastern in its plan; the outer enclosure's [propylaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propylaea) led to a 20-by-9-metre (66 by 30 ft) podium through a portico of which the bases of the columns survives.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidz_IBAwAAQBAJpgPA214_214\]-157]

  &#x20;The peristyle [cella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cella) opened onto an outdoor altar.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidz_IBAwAAQBAJpgPA214_214\]-157]

- The **[Temple of Al-Lat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_of_Al-Lat)** is largely ruined with only a podium, a few columns and the door frame remaining.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeattiePepper2001\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid9iCfkTe8v2ECpgRA2-PA290_290\]-41]

  &#x20;Inside the compound, a giant lion relief ([Lion of Al-lāt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lion_of_Al-l%C4%81t)) was excavated and in its original form, was a relief protruding from the temple compound's wall.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns2009\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidz_IBAwAAQBAJpgPA217_217\]-158]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarkowski2005\[httpswebarchiveorgweb20171020024412httpwwwpcmauweduplfileadminpamPAM_2004_XVI248pdf_473\]-160]

- The ruined **Temple of Baal-hamon** was located on the top of Jabal al-Muntar hill which oversees the spring of Efqa.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDowney1977\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksiddKpnNw5Zj7cCpgPA21_21\]-161]

  &#x20;Constructed in AD 89, it consisted of a cella and a vestibule with two columns.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDowney1977\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksiddKpnNw5Zj7cCpgPA21_21\]-161]

  &#x20;The temple had a defensive tower attached to it;

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDowney1977\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksiddKpnNw5Zj7cCpgPA22_22\]-162]

  &#x20;a [mosaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosaic) depicting the sanctuary was excavated and it revealed that both the cella and the vestibule were decorated with [merlons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merlon).

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDowney1977\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksiddKpnNw5Zj7cCpgPA22_22\]-162]

#### Other buildings

- The **[Monumental Arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monumental_Arch_of_Palmyra)** was built in the third century and destroyed in 2015 by IS.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbdulkarim2020115-154]

- The **Great Colonnade** was Palmyra's 1.1-kilometre-long (0.68 mi) main street; most of the columns date to the second century AD and each is 9.50 metres (31.2 ft) high.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrawford1990\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidgGMc04ge4yoCpgPA123_123\]-28]

- The **Funerary Temple no. 86** (also known as the House Tomb) is located at the western end of the Great Colonnade.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGawlikowski2005\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidoZcr7SzzVYYCpgPA55_55\]-30]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTECasule2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidQNGyWSx8U34CpgPA103_103\]-163]

  &#x20;It was built in the third century AD and has a portico of six columns and vine patterns carvings.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA67_67\]-63]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDarke2006\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidHuXAHGDSjNICpgPA238_238\]-164]

  &#x20;Inside the chamber, steps leads down to a vault crypt.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDarke2006\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidHuXAHGDSjNICpgPA238_238\]-164]

  &#x20;The shrine might have been connected to the royal family as it is the only tomb inside the city's walls.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA67_67\]-63]

- The **Tetrapylon** was erected during the renovations of Diocletian at the end of the third century.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpeake1996\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidR44VRnNCzAYCpgPA568_568\]-95]

  &#x20;It is a square platform and each corner contains a grouping of four columns.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarterDunstonThomas2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_R-I_Gx5OgQCpgPA208_208\]-39]

  &#x20;Each column group supports a 150-ton cornice and contains a pedestal in its center that originally carried a statue.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarterDunstonThomas2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_R-I_Gx5OgQCpgPA208_208\]-39]

  &#x20;Out of sixteen columns, only one is original while the rest are from reconstruction work by the [Syrian Directorate-General of Antiquities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Directorate-General_of_Antiquities_and_Museums) in 1963, using concrete.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDarke2006\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidHuXAHGDSjNICpgPA238_238\]-164]

  &#x20;The original columns were brought from Egypt and carved out of pink granite.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarterDunstonThomas2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_R-I_Gx5OgQCpgPA208_208\]-39]

  &#x20;IS damaged the structure in 2017.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbdulkarim2020119-165]

- The **[Walls of Palmyra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walls_of_Palmyra)** started in the first century as a protective wall containing gaps where the surrounding mountains formed natural barriers; it encompassed the residential areas, the gardens and the oasis.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJuchniewicz2013194-24]

  &#x20;After 273, Aurelian erected the rampart known as the wall of Diocletian;

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJuchniewicz2013194-24]

  &#x20;it enclosed about 80 hectares, a much smaller area than the original pre-273 city.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPollard2000\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid2ijMkjyG3cQCpgPA298_298\]-166]

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTESouthern2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidwnTOBAAAQBAJpgPA142_142\]-167]

### Destruction by IS

See also: [Destruction of cultural heritage by the Islamic State § Palmyra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Destruction_of_cultural_heritage_by_the_Islamic_State#Palmyra)

Ancient Palmyra and its monuments were severely damaged during the Syrian Civil War, which began in 2011. Fighting between the Syrian army and opposition forces took a toll on the site, particularly as several sections were used as military bases. In 2015, the underground Tomb of the Three Brothers and parts of the North Necropolis were used for military purposes, while in 2016 a Russian military camp was established at the site.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmidt-Colinet202021-168] The main damage was caused by militants of IS, which occupied Palmyra on two occasions: first from May 2015 to March 2016, and again from December 2016 to March 2017.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbdulkarim2020115–120-169] The table below provides an overview of the most important monuments severely damaged or destroyed during the war.



Bel's temple entrance arch remains after the destruction of the cella

| Monument | Damage and aftermath |
| --- | --- |
| Artifacts in the Palmyra Archaeological Museum | IS smashed around 200 artifacts with hammers, including The statue of Al-lāt (Athena) whose head was left in pieces. On 23 May 2015 IS damaged the [Lion of Al-lāt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lion_of_Al-l%C4%81t) statue. Not all pieces were completely destroyed and the Lion of Al-lāt was restored.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbdulkarim2020115,_118-170][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJeffries2015-171] |
| Temple of Baalshamin | Completely destroyed by IS on 23 August 2015.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnesco2015-172] |
| Temple of Bel | IS destroyed the cella of the temple on 30 August 2015. The temple's exterior walls and entrance arch remain.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connor2015-173][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETharoorMaruf2016-174] |
| Valley of the Tombs | In September 2015, it became apparent that IS had destroyed several tower and underground tombs, including the [Tower of Elahbel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tower_of_Elahbel).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShaheenSwannLevett2015-175] The damaged tombs included the Tombs of Atenatan and Iamliku, Tomb no. 71, and the tomb of Julius Aurelius Borema, among others.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmidt-Colinet202021,_22-176] |
| The Monumental Arch | IS damaged the arch in October 2015.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMakieh2015-177] |
| The Tetrapylon and the Roman Theatre | On 20 January 2017 news emerged that IS damaged the façade of the theatre and toppled 12 of the 16 columns that formed the tetrapylon.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbdulkarim2020120-178][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShaheen2017-179] The damage to the façade of the Roman theatre was less serious.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaqdisi2017-180] |
| The Southeastern Burials | Several tombs were damaged by IS in 2017 including the Aviation Tomb.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmidt-Colinet202021-168] |

#### Restoration

On 21 October 2015, using images gathered and released into the public domain by the Syrian internet advocate [Bassel Khartabil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bassel_Khartabil) between 2005 and 2012, [Creative Commons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creative_Commons) started the [New Palmyra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NEWPALMYRA) project, an online repository of three-dimensional models of the city's monuments.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBusta2015-181][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreenberg2015-182] In 2017, two Palmyrene funerary busts restored by the [ISCR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Istituto_Superiore_per_la_Conservazione_ed_il_Restauro) were sent back to Syria.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaurentiIdelson2018138-183] The restoration of the Lion of Al-lāt took two months and the statue was displayed on 1 October 2017; it will remain in the [National Museum of Damascus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Damascus).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMakiehPerryMerriman2017-184]

Prior to the fall of Al-Assad regime in 2024, the Russians were the main foreign partner involved in the restoration of Palmyra.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESoloveva20228-185] To gather data that can aid the restoration of the damaged monuments, the site was researched in September 2016 and September 2019 by teams of the RAC IHMC RAS.[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuzevanovTushkinaTrynova202217-187] In autumn 2021 Russia and Syria agreed to launch a joint-project to restore the Monumental Arch under the scientific management of the RAC IHMC RAS, and the project was launched in 2022.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESoloveva20228,_9-188] The teams conducted several studies researching the construction of the arch as a first step in restoring it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuzevanovTushkinaTrynova202217-187] Work on the arch was limited to data collection and inventarisation but no restoration took place.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKannawi2025-189] In 2022 the Efqa spring site was reopened after being rehabilitated by the Russian-Syrian teams.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESartre-Fauriat2024590-190] Following the fall of Al-Assad regime end of 2024 the UNESCO undertook steps to restore the site in cooperation with the Syrian government, [ALIPH](https://nl.wikipedia.org/wiki/ALIPH) and the [Aga Khan Trust for Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aga_Khan_Trust_for_Culture). As of June 2026, work was limited to surveys and damage assessments.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUNESCO_World_Heritage_Committee202647-191]

## History

Former spring, with steps

[Efqa Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efqa_Spring), which dried up in 1994

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESouthern2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidwnTOBAAAQBAJpgPA18_18\]-192]

The area has a rich history of human habitation, with evidence of Paleolithic settlements.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaniharaSakaguchiAkazawa1978-193] The site was inhabited during several periods in the [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) (10,000–4500 BC). In the area of Tell ez-Zor, an artificial hill in the vicinity of the Efqa Spring, traces of human settlement dates as far back as the [Prepottery Neolithic A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Pottery_Neolithic_A) period, with a three-unit architectural complex dating to the [Prepottery Neolithic B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Pottery_Neolithic_B) period.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEal-MaqdissiIshaq201742-194] In the [Efqa Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efqa_Spring) site, a Neolithic settlement existed,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpeake1996\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidR44VRnNCzAYCpgPA565_565\]-195] with [stone tools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stone_tool#Neolithic_industries) dated to 7500 BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEColledgeWiesehöfer2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidAIgdBAAAQBAJpgPA566_566\]-196]

### Bronze Age

[Archaeological sounding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stratigraphy) in the tell beneath the Temple of Bel uncovered a [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age) mud-brick structure built around 2500 BC, followed by different Bronze Age and Iron Age structures.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEal-Maqdissi2010140-197] The city entered the historical record around 2000 BC, when Puzur-Ishtar the Tadmorean (Palmyrene) agreed to a contract at an [Assyrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyria#Dynasty_of_Puzur-Ashur_I,_2025%E2%80%931809_BC,_Old_Assyrian_Empire) trading colony in [Kultepe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kultepe).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEColledgeWiesehöfer2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidAIgdBAAAQBAJpgPA566_566\]-196] It was mentioned next in the [Mari tablets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mari,_Syria#Mari_tablets) as a stop for trade caravans and nomadic tribes, such as the [Suteans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suteans),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA18_18\]-61] and was conquered along with its region by [Yahdun-Lim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahdun-Lim) of Mari.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith195638-198] King [Shamshi-Adad I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shamshi-Adad_I) of the [Kingdom of Upper Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Upper_Mesopotamia) passed through the area on his way to the [Mediterranean](19006.md) at the beginning of the 18th century BC;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELiverani2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid0d1JAgAAQBAJpgPA234_234\]-199] by then, Palmyra was the easternmost point of the kingdom of [Qatna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qatna),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsmail2002325-200] and it was attacked by the Suteans who paralyzed the traffic along the trade routes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEvan_Koppen201587-201] Palmyra was next mentioned in a 13th-century BC tablet discovered at [Emar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emar), which recorded the names of two "Tadmorean" witnesses.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA18_18\]-61]

### Iron Age

At the beginning of the 11th century BC, King [Tiglath-Pileser I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiglath-Pileser_I) of Assyria recorded his defeat of the "Arameans" of "Tadmar";[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA18_18\]-61] according to the king, Palmyra was part of the land of Amurru.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2009\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidAwwNS0diXP4CpgPA686_686\]-202] The [Hebrew Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible) ([Second Book of Chronicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Books_of_Chronicles) 8\:4) records a city by the name "Tadmor" as a desert city built (or fortified) by King [Solomon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon) of [Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Israel_\(united_monarchy\));[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShahîd1995\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidBEvEV9OVzacCpgPA173_173\]-203] Flavius [Josephus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josephus) mentions the Greek name "Palmyra", attributing its founding to Solomon in Book VIII of his *[Antiquities of the Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiquities_of_the_Jews)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillar1993\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidIA-YlZqHv90CpgPA320_320\]-156] Later Arabic traditions attribute the city's founding to Solomon's [Jinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jinn).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShahîd2002\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidpfwAG3-rpzcCpgPA282_282\]-204] The association of Palmyra with Solomon is a conflation of "Tadmor" and a city built by Solomon in [Judea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judea) and known as "Tamar" in the [Books of Kings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Books_of_Kings) (1 Kings 9\:18).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA276_276\]-155] The biblical description of "Tadmor" and its buildings does not fit archaeological findings in Palmyra, which was a small settlement during Solomon's reign in the 10th century BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA276_276\]-155]

With the rise of Aramean power in the Levant, the city became the eastern border of [Aram-Damascus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aram-Damascus) which was conquered by the [Neo-Assyrian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Assyrian_Empire) in 732 BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESader2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidsW_AAgAAQBAJpgPA24_24\]-205] The [Elephantine Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elephantine_papyri), a diaspora community established between 650 and 550 BC in Egypt, might have come from Palmyra.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEvan_der_Toorn2019\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidmpKsDwAAQBAJpgPA143_143\]-206] [Papyrus Amherst 63](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papyrus_Amherst_63) indicates that the ancestors of the Elephantine Jews were [Samarians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaria). The historian [Karel van der Toorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karel_van_der_Toorn) suggested that these ancestors took refuge in Judea after the destruction of their kingdom by [Sargon II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sargon_II) of Assyria in 721 BC, then had to leave Judea after [Sennacherib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sennacherib) devastated the land in 701 BC and headed to Palmyra. This scenario can explain the usage of Aramaic by the Elephantine Jews, and Papyrus Amherst 63, while not mentioning Palmyra, refers to a "fortress of palms" that is located near a spring on a trade route in the fringes of the desert, making Palmyra a plausible candidate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEvan_der_Toorn2019\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidmpKsDwAAQBAJpgPA144_144,_145\]-207]

### Hellenistic and Roman periods

Stone inscribed with Greek letters

The inscription mentioning king *Epiphanes*

During the [Hellenistic period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_period) under the [Seleucids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seleucids) (between 312 and 64 BC), Palmyra became a prosperous settlement owing allegiance to the Seleucid king.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA276_276\]-155][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA52_52\]-208] Evidence for Palmyra's urbanisation in the Hellenistic period is rare; an important piece is the [Laghman II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramaic_Inscription_of_Laghman) inscription found in [Laghman](457257.md), modern [Afghanistan](737.md), and commissioned by the Indian emperor [Ashoka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashoka) c. 250 BC. The reading is contested, but according to semitologist [André Dupont-Sommer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Dupont-Sommer), the inscription records the distance to "Tdmr" (Palmyra).[^cite_note-215][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaizer2017\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidC7Q1DwAAQBAJpgPA33_33\],_34-210] In 217 BC, a Palmyrene force led by Zabdibel joined the army of King [Antiochus III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiochus_III) in the [Battle of Raphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Raphia) which ended in a Seleucid defeat by [Ptolemaic Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemaic_Kingdom).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA278_278\]-47] In the middle of the Hellenistic era, Palmyra, formerly south of the al-Qubur wadi, began to expand beyond its northern bank.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZuchowska2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidFgML1EdUiWUCpgPA231_231\]-27] By the late second century BC, the tower tombs in the Palmyrene Valley of Tombs and the city temples (most notably, the temples of [Baalshamin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baalshamin), Al-lāt and the Hellenistic temple) began to be built.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA63_63\]-26][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA278_278\]-47][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA276_276\]-155] A fragmentary inscription in Greek from the Temple of Bel's foundations mentions a king titled Epiphanes, a title used by the Seleucid kings.[^cite_note-217][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrainger1997\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksideqxipjRXCf4CpgPA759lpgPA759_759\]-218]

In 64 BC, the [Roman Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Republic) conquered the Seleucid kingdom, and the Roman general [Pompey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pompey) established the [province of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_\(Roman_province\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA278_278\]-47] Palmyra was left independent,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA278_278\]-47] trading with Rome and [Parthia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthian_empire) but belonging to neither.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElton1996\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidN0jAQ1oP3_wCpgPA90_90\]-219] The earliest known inscription in Palmyrene is dated to around 44 BC;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA19_19\]-50] Palmyra was still a minor [sheikhdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikhdom), offering water to caravans which occasionally took the desert route on which it was located.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBall2002\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid73-JAgAAQBAJpgPA74_74\]-220] However, according to [Appian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appian), Palmyra was wealthy enough for [Mark Antony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Antony) to send a force to conquer it in 41 BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElton1996\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidN0jAQ1oP3_wCpgPA90_90\]-219] The Palmyrenes evacuated to Parthian lands beyond the eastern bank of the [Euphrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euphrates),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElton1996\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidN0jAQ1oP3_wCpgPA90_90\]-219] which they prepared to defend.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA19_19\]-50]

#### Autonomous Palmyrene region

Temple ruins

Cella of the Temple of Bel (destroyed in 2015)



Temple of Baal-Shamin (destroyed in 2015)

Well-preserved Roman amphitheater

Palmyra's theater (damaged in 2017)

Ruins, with arches and columns

The monumental arch in the eastern section of Palmyra's colonnade (destroyed in 2015)

Palmyra became part of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) when it was conquered and paid tribute early in the reign of [Tiberius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiberius), around 14 AD.[^cite_note-222][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA278_278\]-47][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwell2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_UR8AgAAQBAJpgPT34_34\]-223] The Romans included Palmyra in the province of Syria,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwell2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidDQgmOZlsEWcCpgPA34_34\]-221] and defined the region's boundaries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwell2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidDQgmOZlsEWcCpgPA41_41\]-224] [Pliny the Elder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pliny_the_Elder) asserted that both the Palmyrene and [Emesene](77533.md#Emesene_dynasty_and_Roman_rule) regions were contiguous;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeyrig1959189–190-225] a marker at the Palmyrene's southwestern border was found in 1936 by [Daniel Schlumberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Schlumberger) at [Qasr al-Hayr al-Gharbi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qasr_al-Hayr_al-Gharbi), dating from the reign of [Hadrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrian) or one of his successors, which marked the boundary between the two regions.[^cite_note-227][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchlumberger193943,_66-228][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA284_284\]-229] This boundary probably ran northwards to Khirbet al-Bilaas on [Jabal al-Bilas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabal_al-Bilas) where another marker, laid by the Roman governor [Silanus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quintus_Caecilius_Metellus_Creticus_Silanus), has been found, 75 kilometres (47 mi) northwest of Palmyra, probably marking a boundary with the territory of [Epiphania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hama).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESeyrig1959190-230][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwell2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidDQgmOZlsEWcCpgPA41_41\]-224] Meanwhile, Palmyra's eastern border extended to the Euphrates valley.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA284_284\]-229] This region included numerous villages subordinate to the center,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeyer2013275-231] including large settlements such as [al-Qaryatayn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaryatayn).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA124_124\]-232] The Roman imperial period brought great prosperity to the city, which enjoyed a privileged status under the empire—retaining much of its internal autonomy,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA278_278\]-47] being ruled by a council,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA127_127\]-233] and incorporating many Greek city-state ([polis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polis)) institutions into its government.[^cite_note-235][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA122_122\]-234]

The earliest Palmyrene text attesting a Roman presence in the city dates to 18 AD, when the Roman general [Germanicus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanicus) tried to develop a friendly relationship with Parthia; he sent the Palmyrene Alexandros to [Mesene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Characene), a Parthian vassal kingdom.[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA20_20\]-239] This was followed by the arrival of the Roman legion [Legio X Fretensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legio_X_Fretensis) the following year.[^cite_note-241][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDąbrowa1993\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidyH4MQy0wrbcCpgPA12_12\]-240] Roman authority was minimal during the first century AD, although tax collectors were resident,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElton1996\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidN0jAQ1oP3_wCpgPA91_91\]-242] and a road connecting Palmyra and [Sura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sura,_Syria) was built in AD 75.[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElton1996\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidN0jAQ1oP3_wCpgPA92_92\]-243] The Romans used Palmyrene soldiers,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESouthern2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidecfiAAAAQBAJpgPA25_25\]-245] but (unlike typical Roman cities) no local [magistrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_magistrate) or [prefects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prefect#Ancient_Rome) are recorded in the city.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElton1996\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidN0jAQ1oP3_wCpgPA92_92\]-243] Palmyra saw intensive construction during the first century, including the city's first walled fortifications,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrijvers1976\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidmuQ3AAAAIAAJpgPA3_3\]-246] and the Temple of Bel (completed and dedicated in 32 AD).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillar1993\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidIA-YlZqHv90CpgPA323_323\]-150] During the first century Palmyra developed from a minor desert caravan station into a leading trading center,[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBall2002\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid73-JAgAAQBAJpgPA74_74\]-220] with Palmyrene merchants establishing colonies in surrounding trade centers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA20_20\]-239]

Palmyrene trade reached its acme during the second century,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA22_22\]-249] aided by two factors; the first was a trade route built by Palmyrenes,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung2003\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidE5yCAgAAQBAJpgPA124_124\]-20] and protected by garrisons at major locations, including a garrison in [Dura-Europos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dura-Europos) manned in 117 AD.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA145_145\]-250] The second was the [Roman conquest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabia_Petraea#Roman_conquest) of the [Nabataean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabataean_kingdom) capital [Petra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petra) in 106,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA278_278\]-47] shifting control over southern trade routes of the [Arabian Peninsula](47858.md) from the Nabataeans to Palmyra.[^cite_note-252][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA278_278\]-47] In 129 Palmyra was visited by [Hadrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadrian), who named it "Hadriane Palmyra" and made it a [free city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_city_\(antiquity\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA279_279\]-253][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA21_21\]-254] Hadrian promoted [Hellenism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenization) throughout the empire,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA25_25\]-255] and Palmyra's urban expansion was modeled on that of Greece.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA25_25\]-255] This led to new projects, including the theatre, the colonnade and the Temple of Nabu.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA25_25\]-255] Roman garrisons are first attested in Palmyra in 167, when the cavalry [Ala I Thracum Herculiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auxilia#Alae) was moved to the city.[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERaschke1978878-259] By the end of the second century, urban development diminished after the city's building projects peaked.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA26_26\]-260]

In the 190s, Palmyra was assigned to the province of [Phoenice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenice_\(Roman_province\)), newly created by the [Severan dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severan_dynasty).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwell2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidDQgmOZlsEWcCpgPA27_27\]-261] Toward the end of the second century, Palmyra began a steady transition from a traditional Greek city-state to a monarchy due to the increasing militarization of the city and the deteriorating economic situation;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESartre2005\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidMNSyT_PuYVMCpgPA512_512\]-262] the Severan ascension to the imperial throne in Rome played a major role in Palmyra's transition:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA26_26\]-260]

- The Severan-led [Roman–Parthian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman%E2%80%93Parthian_Wars), from 194 to 217, influenced regional security and affected the city's trade.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA28_28\]-263]
