Palmyra's first scholarly description appeared in a 1695 paper in the *Philosophical Transactions* of the [Royal Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Society) by [William Hallifax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Hallifax) followed by a 1696 book by [Abednego Seller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abednego_Seller).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalker2017120,_125-516] In 1751, an expedition led by [Robert Wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Wood_\(antiquarian\)) and [James Dawkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Dawkins_\(antiquarian\)) studied Palmyra's architecture.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA7_7\]-517] French artist and architect [Louis-François Cassas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis-Fran%C3%A7ois_Cassas) conducted an extensive survey of the city's monuments in 1785, publishing over a hundred drawings of Palmyra's civic buildings and tombs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmidt-Colinet201970-518] Palmyra was photographed for the first time in 1864 by Louis Vignes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmidt-Colinet201971-519] In 1882, the [Palmyra Tariff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyra_Tariff), an inscribed stone slab from AD 137 in Greek and Palmyrene detailing import and export taxation, was discovered by prince [Semyon Semyonovich Abamelik-Lazarev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semyon_Semyonovich_Abamelik-Lazarev) in the Tariff Court.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGawlikowski2011415-520] It has been described by the historian [John F. Matthews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Matthews) as "one of the most important single items of evidence for the economic life of any part of the Roman Empire".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHealey2009\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidOp8VDAAAQBAJpgPA164_164\]-521] In 1901, the slab was gifted by the Ottoman Sultan [Abdul Hamid II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Hamid_II) to the Russian Tsar and is now in the [Hermitage Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermitage_Museum) in [Saint Petersburg](24320051.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGawlikowski2011416-522]



Excavations at Palmyra, 1962, Polish archaeologist [Kazimierz Michałowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazimierz_Micha%C5%82owski)

Palmyra's first excavations were conducted in 1902 by [Otto Puchstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_Puchstein) and in 1917 by [Theodor Wiegand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodor_Wiegand).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA12_12\]-392] In 1929, French general director of antiquities of Syria and Lebanon Henri Seyrig began large-scale excavation of the site;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA12_12\]-392] interrupted by World War II, it resumed soon after the war's end.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA12_12\]-392] Seyrig started with the Temple of Bel in 1929 and between 1939 and 1940 he excavated the Agora.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrijvers1976\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidmuQ3AAAAIAAJpgPA4_4\]-393] Daniel Schlumberger conducted excavations in the Palmyrene northwest countryside in 1934 and 1935 where he studied different local sanctuaries in the Palmyrene villages.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrijvers1976\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidmuQ3AAAAIAAJpgPA4_4\]-393] From 1954 to 1956, a Swiss expedition organized by [UNESCO](21786641.md) excavated the Temple of Baalshamin.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA12_12\]-392] Since 1958, the site has been excavated by the Syrian Directorate-General of Antiquities,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDarke2010\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidjv2jHT_GRe0CpgPA257_257\]-391] and Polish expeditions of the [Polish Centre of Mediterranean Archaeology University of Warsaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Centre_of_Mediterranean_Archaeology),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGawlikowski2013\[httpsdigiubuni-heidelbergdediglitstudia_palmyrenskie20130015image_13\]-523] led consequtively by [Kazimierz Michałowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazimierz_Micha%C5%82owski), [Anna Sadurska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anna_Sadurska), [Michał Gawlikowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Gawlikowski) and [Grzegorz Majcherek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grzegorz_Majcherek).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMyśliwiec201814-524] The stratigraphic sounding beneath the Temple of Bel was conducted in 1967 by [Robert du Mesnil du Buisson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_du_Mesnil_du_Buisson),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBielińska1997\[httpdigiubuni-heidelbergdediglitstudia_palmyrenskie19970044_44\]-96] who also discovered the Temple of Baal-hamon in the 1970s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDowney1977\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksiddKpnNw5Zj7cCpgPA21_21\]-161] In 1980, the historic site including the necropolis outside the walls was declared a [World Heritage Site](44940.md) by the UNESCO.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECameronRössler2016\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidWdYoDAAAQBAJpgPA105_105\]-525]

The Polish expedition concentrated its work on the Camp of Diocletian while the Syrian Directorate-General of Antiquities excavated the Temple of Nabu.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrijvers1976\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidmuQ3AAAAIAAJpgPA4_4\]-393] Most of the hypogea were excavated jointly by the Polish expedition and the Syrian Directorate,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDowney1996\[httpjournalscambridgeorgactiondisplayAbstractfromPageonlineaid9116455_469\]-526] while the area of Efqa was excavated by Jean Starcky and [Jafar al-Hassani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jafar_al-Hassani?action=edit\&redlink=1).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrijvers1976\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidmuQ3AAAAIAAJpgPA5_5\]-35] The Palmyrene irrigation system was discovered in 2008 by [Jørgen Christian Meyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C3%B8rgen_Christian_Meyer?action=edit\&redlink=1) who researched the Palmyrene countryside through ground inspections and satellite images.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeyer2017b\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid5D5nDwAAQBAJpgPA30_30\]-527] Most of Palmyra still remains unexplored especially the residential quarters in the north and south while the necropolis has been thoroughly excavated by the Directorate and the Polish expedition.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrijvers1976\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidmuQ3AAAAIAAJpgPA5_5\]-35] Since the initiation of archaeological research in Palmyra, many international teams worked on the site, including expeditions from Japan, France, Poland, The United States, Italy and Norway. Excavation expeditions left Palmyra in 2011 due to the Syrian Civil War.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERajaSeland2025462,_474-528]

[^cite_note-1]: &#x20;[/pæl.ˈmaɪ.rə/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English), [*pal-MY-rə*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key); [Palmyrene Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_Aramaic_language): 𐡶𐡣𐡬𐡥𐡴‎, romanized: *Tadmor*; [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): تَدْمُر, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic): *Tadmur*

[^cite_note-10]: &#x20;The Semitic word T.M.R is the common root for the words that designate palm dates in [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language), [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language), [Ge'ez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ge'ez_language) and other Semitic languages.<sup>[\[5\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurtonen1986\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidt_9NkGsNtcYCpgPA445_445]-6)</sup>\
    Schultens argued that in the Bible (1 Kings 9\:18), the name is written "Tamor" in the text and "Tadmor" in the margin.<sup>[\[6\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEIbn_Šaddād1732\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidFCgVAAAAQAAJpgRA3-PT79lpgRA3-PT79_79]-7)</sup> Schultens considered "Tamor" to be the original name and derived from "Tamar".<sup>[\[7\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTECharnock1859\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsbub_gb_I2BulY4WvsYCpagen210_200]-8)</sup> However, the inclusion of a -*d*- in "Tamar" cannot be explained.<sup>[\[8\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Connor1988\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidx1l_MfEV6kkCpgPA235_235]-9)</sup>

[^cite_note-22]: &#x20;Pliny mentioned that Palmyra was independent, but by AD 70, Palmyra was part of the Roman empire; modern scholars dismiss Pliny's account of Palmyra's political situation, considering it to rely on older accounts dating to the period of [Octavian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octavian), when Palmyra was independent.<sup>[\[19\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwell2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidDQgmOZlsEWcCpgPA44_44]-21)</sup>

[^cite_note-55]: &#x20;Estimates vary from as low as 30,000 to 200,000; the latter number is doubted considering the environment of Palmyra and its hinterland which makes it difficult to provide the population with the necessary foodstuff.<sup>[\[51\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeyer2013270-54)</sup>

[^cite_note-64]: &#x20;E.g for Aramaic: Gaddibol and Yedi'bel.<sup>[\[59\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA67_67]-63)</sup>\
    E.g for Arab: Bene Ma'zin.<sup>[\[59\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA67_67]-63)</sup>\
    E.g for Amorite: Zmr' and Kohen-Nadu.<sup>[\[59\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA67_67]-63)</sup>

[^cite_note-70]: &#x20;These criteria are: dispersion in a wide geographical range; orientation towards a homeland through the usage of own language and reference to a distinct origin and pantheon; the usage of own language and origin to preserve a distinct identity amongst host societies.<sup>[\[64\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeland2013382-69)</sup>

[^cite_note-84]: &#x20;The Phylai are the Bene Mita, Komare, Mattabol, Ma'zin and Claudia.<sup>[\[77\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA24_24]-83)</sup>

[^cite_note-88]: &#x20;In general, a civic tribe (Phyle) is a collection of people chosen from the collective population and ascribed a deity as a tribal ancestor, then assigned a territory for them to reside in. The Phylai were united by their citizenship instead of origin.<sup>[\[80\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeier1990\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidEx8HGWKViYQCpgPA60_60]-87)</sup>

[^cite_note-89]: &#x20;The clans might have gathered under the name of the four tribes causing them to disappear.<sup>[\[78\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDirven1999\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_LfXg2r6FT0CpgPA25_25]-85)</sup>

[^cite_note-100]: &#x20;E.g. by the second century AD, Palmyrene goddess [Al-lāt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-l%C4%81t) was portrayed in the style of the Greek goddess [Athena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athena), and named Athena-Al-lāt. However, this assimilation of Al-lāt to Athena did not extend beyond iconography.<sup>[\[90\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTETeixidor1979\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidTccUAAAAIAAJpgPR62_62]-99)</sup>

[^cite_note-110]: &#x20;In the Hellenistic tradition, the agora was the center of athletic, artistic, spiritual and political life of the city.<sup>[\[99\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVasudevan1995\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid74JI2UlcU8ACpgPA66_66]-109)</sup>

[^cite_note-130]: &#x20;There are hints of Greek training; the names of three Greeks who worked on the construction of the Temple of Bel are known through inscriptions, including a probably Greek architect named Alexandras (Αλεξάνδρας).<sup>[\[116\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA54_54]-127)</sup><sup>[\[117\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmidt-Colinet1997157-128)</sup> However, some Palmyrenes adopted Greco-Roman names and native citizens with the name Alexander are attested in the city.<sup>[\[118\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYon2002\[httpsbooksopeneditionorgifpo3763_59,_10]-129)</sup>

[^cite_note-141]: &#x20;The historian Rudolf Fellmann suggested that this building was the royal palace.<sup>[\[128\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFellmann1987136-140)</sup>

[^cite_note-186]: &#x20;The Rescue Archeology Center of the Institute for the History of Material Culture of the [Russian Academy of Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Academy_of_Sciences)

[^cite_note-215]: &#x20;According to the reading of Dupont-Sommer, Palmyra is separated by two hundreds "bows" from Laghman; In the inscription, the word used to indicate bow is "QŠTN", and Dupont-Sommer asserted that it is an Aramaic word denoting a unit to measure a distance of 15 to 20 kilometres.<sup>[\[195\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDupont-Sommer1970163-209)</sup> [Franz Altheim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franz_Altheim) and Ruth Altheim-Stiehl read three hundred instead of two hundred bows; they equated it with the Vedic unit of measurement [yojona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yojana), c. 12 kilometres, which would result in a number close to the actual 3800 kilometres distance between Laghman and Palmyra.<sup>[\[196\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaizer2017\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidC7Q1DwAAQBAJpgPA33_33],_34-210)</sup> The linguist Helmut Humbach criticized the reading of Dupont-Sommer and considered his claims regarding the distance to have no validation.<sup>[\[197\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacDowallTaddei1978192-211)</sup> In the Aramaic alphabet, the letters "r" and "d" share an identical character;<sup>[\[198\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaizer2017\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidC7Q1DwAAQBAJpgPA34_34]-212)</sup> [Jean de Menasce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_de_Menasce) read the city's name "Trmd" and identified it with [Termez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Termez) on the [Oxus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amu_Darya) river.<sup>[\[199\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMukherjee200011-213)</sup> The linguist [Franz Rosenthal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franz_Rosenthal) also contested the reading of Dupont-Sommer and considered that the inscription refers to an estate called "Trmn".<sup>[\[200\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTERosenthal197899-214)</sup> Historian [Bratindra Nath Mukherjee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B._N._Mukherjee) rejected the readings of both Dupont-Sommer and de Menasce; he contested the large value attributed to "bow", considering it a small unit. The historian also rejected the reading of Tdmr and Trmd as referring to a city; in the view of Mukherjee, the name, whether Tdmr or Trmd refers to the rock on which the inscription was carved itself.<sup>[\[199\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMukherjee200011-213)</sup>

[^cite_note-217]: &#x20;The inscription is in bad shape but the letters' form, especially the four-branched [sigma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigma), indicate that it is one of the earliest inscriptions from Palmyra, dating to the beginning of the first century AD or the former first century BC. Seyrig concluded that it is futile to identify the king as the title Epiphanes was borne by many Seleucid kings, the last of them, [Antiochus XII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiochus_XII_Dionysus), died in 82 BC. Even then, according to Seyrig, the date is too high for the form of the letters. Seyrig suggested a king of [Commagene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commagene) or, more likely, a Parthian king.<sup>[\[201\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeyrig1939322,_323-216)</sup>

[^cite_note-222]: &#x20;The attribution of Palmyra annexation to Tiberius was supported by Seyrig and became the most influential. However, other dates have been suggested ranging from as early as [Pompey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pompey)'s era to as late as [Vespasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vespasian)'s reign.<sup>[\[205\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwell2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidDQgmOZlsEWcCpgPA34_34]-221)</sup>

[^cite_note-227]: &#x20;Inscription reproduced:<sup>[\[209\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchlumberger193964-226)</sup>\
    &#x20;      *Fin*\[*es*]\
    &#x20;      *inteṛ*\
    *Hadriano*\[*s*]\
    *Palmyrenos*\
    &#x20;          *et*\
    \[*He*]*ṃesenos*

[^cite_note-235]: &#x20;The exact year for when Palmyra first made use of some Greek institutions is not known; the evidence that specifically identify Palmyra as a polis is not extensive, and the earliest known reference is an inscription dated to AD 51, written in Palmyrene and Greek, mentioning the "City of the Palmyrenes" in its Greek section.<sup>[\[216\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA122_122]-234)</sup>

[^cite_note-238]: &#x20;Despite his Greek name, Alexandros was probably a native Palmyrene.<sup>[\[217\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA226_226]-236)</sup>\
    There is no evidence that Germanicus visited Palmyra.<sup>[\[218\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA24_24]-237)</sup>

[^cite_note-241]: &#x20;The legion was part of Germanicus' eastern campaign and was not stationed in the city as a garrison.<sup>[\[220\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDąbrowa1993\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidyH4MQy0wrbcCpgPA12_12]-240)</sup>

[^cite_note-244]: &#x20;Commissioned by [Traianus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcus_Ulpius_Traianus_\(senior\)).<sup>[\[222\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEElton1996\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidN0jAQ1oP3_wCpgPA92_92]-243)</sup>

[^cite_note-248]: &#x20;The transformation already began in the first century BC.<sup>[\[225\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwell2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidDQgmOZlsEWcCpgPA36_36]-247)</sup>

[^cite_note-252]: &#x20;Although Palmyra benefiting from the annexation of Petra is a mainstream view, Palmyra's trade was mostly with the East, while Petra's trade counted on southern Arabia. In addition to the fact that Palmyra and Petra traded in different articles, hence the annexation of Petra might have not had a real effect on Palmyra's trade.<sup>[\[228\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYoung2003\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidE5yCAgAAQBAJpgPA125_125]-251)</sup>

[^cite_note-258]: &#x20;The Ala I Thracum Herculiana was a *milliaria*.<sup>[\[232\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDąbrowa1979\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidd2DmN0xH5-gCpgPA235_235]-256)</sup> Generally, a *milliaria* consisted of a thousand horsemen.<sup>[\[233\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESidebothamHenseNouwens2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidEyemu-_MncwCpgPA354_354]-257)</sup>

[^cite_note-273]: &#x20;No evidence exist for Roman units serving in the ranks of Odaenathus; whether Roman soldiers fought under Odaenathus or not is a matter of speculation.<sup>[\[247\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESouthern2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidwnTOBAAAQBAJpgPA60_60]-272)</sup>

[^cite_note-284]: &#x20;The Mesopotamian Jewish population was regarded by the Palmyrenes as loyal to the Persians.<sup>[\[257\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDubnov1968\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidLLCXomFNU3cCpgPA151_151]-283)</sup>

[^cite_note-290]: &#x20;The first decisive evidence for the use of this title for Odaenathus is an inscription dated to 271, posthumously describing Odaenathus as "King of Kings".<sup>[\[245\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA177_177]-270)</sup><sup>[\[261\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStoneman1994\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8kLFfE1qPhICpgPA78_78]-288)</sup> Known inscriptions dating to his reign address him as king. However, Odaenathus' son Hairan I, is directly attested as "King of Kings" during his lifetime. Hairan I was proclaimed by his father as co-ruler and was assassinated during the same assassination incident that took the life of Odaenathus and it is unlikely that Odaenathus was simply a king while his son held the King of Kings title.<sup>[\[262\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESouthern2008\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidwnTOBAAAQBAJpgPA72_72]-289)</sup>

[^cite_note-310]: &#x20;Claudius died in August 270, shortly before Zenobia's invasion of Egypt.<sup>[\[277\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatson2004\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidkJ2JAgAAQBAJpgPA62_62]-305)</sup>

[^cite_note-316]: &#x20;[Scholarly opinion is divided](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_Empire#Evaluation_and_legacy) as to whether this was a declaration of independence or a usurpation of the Roman throne.<sup>[\[284\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBall2002\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid73-JAgAAQBAJpgPA82_82]-313)</sup><sup>[\[285\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhittow2010\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidGSmrBAAAQBAJpgPT154_77]-314)</sup><sup>[\[286\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith_II2013\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidh5cMho6zFckCpgPA180_180]-315)</sup>
